\phi=({\tau_{1}}^{\prime}E_{1}+{\tau_{2}}^{\prime}E_{2}+{\tau_{3}}^{\prime}E_{3})^{\sim}\in\mathfrak{h}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
and set
\phi_{\alpha_{1}}:=(\tau_{1}E_{1}+\tau_{2}E_{2}+\tau_{3}E_{3})^{\sim}
, then from
B_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\phi_{\alpha_{1}},\phi)=\alpha_{1}(\phi)
, we have

\displaystyle\tau_{1}=0,\,\,\tau_{2}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{3},\,\,\tau_{3}=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}.

Hence we have
\phi_{\alpha_{1}}=(1/3)(E_{2}-E_{3})^{\sim}
, and similarly
\phi_{\alpha_{2}}=(1/3)(E_{3}-E_{1})^{\sim}
.

With above, we see that

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1})

\displaystyle=B_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\phi_{\alpha_{1}},\phi_{\alpha_{1}})=\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\left(\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}+\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\right)\cdot\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\right)\right)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{3},

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\phi_{\alpha_{1}},\phi_{\alpha_{2}})=\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\right)\cdot\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{6},

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\phi_{\alpha_{2}},\phi_{\alpha_{2}})=\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\left(\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\right)\cdot\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\right)+\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\right)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}

Hence, since we have

\displaystyle\cos\theta_{12}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}{\sqrt{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1})(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})}}=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2},

we can draw the Dynkin diagram.
∎

5.
The group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
and the root system, the Dynkin diagram of the Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}})^{C}
of the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}

Let
\mathfrak{P}^{C}
be the Freudenthal
C
-vector space. As in
(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, we consider a
C
-vector space
(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
which is defined by replacing
\mathfrak{C}
with
R
in
\mathfrak{P}^{C}
:

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}:=(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\oplus(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\oplus C\oplus C.

We consider the following groups
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
which are respectively defined by replacing
\mathfrak{P}^{C}
with
(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
in the groups
(E_{7})^{C},E_{7}
:

\displaystyle(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}

\displaystyle=\left\{\alpha\in\mbox{\rm{Iso}}_{C}((\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\alpha(P\times Q)\alpha^{-1}=\alpha P\times\alpha Q\right\},

\displaystyle E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\alpha\in\mbox{\rm{Iso}}_{C}((\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\alpha(P\times Q)\alpha^{-1}=\alpha P\times\alpha Q,\langle\alpha P,\alpha Q\rangle=\langle P,Q\rangle\right\}.

The group
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
is a compact Lie group as the closed subgroup of the unitary group
U(14)=U((\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})=\{\alpha\in\mbox{\rm{Iso}}_{C}((\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})\,|\,\langle\alpha P,\alpha Q\rangle=\langle P,Q\rangle\}
and the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is its complexification of
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
.

Here, as in the Lie algebras
{\mathfrak{e}_{7}}^{C},\mathfrak{e}_{7}
of the groups
{E_{7}}^{C},E_{7}
, the Lie algebras
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
of the groups
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
are given by:

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}

\displaystyle=\left\{\varPhi(\phi,A,B,\nu)\in\mbox{\rm{Hom}}_{C}((\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\phi\in(\mathfrak{e}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},A,B\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},\nu\in C\right\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\varPhi(\phi,A,-\tau A,\nu)\in\mbox{\rm{Hom}}_{C}((\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\phi\in\mathfrak{e}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}},A\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},\nu\in i\mbox{\boldmath$R$}\right\},

respectively.

In particular, we have
\dim_{C}((\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})=8+6\times 2+1=21
and
\dim(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})=8+12+1=21
.

Hereafter, we will determine the type of the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
as Lie algebras.

Let
\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
be the Lie algebra of the symplectic group

Sp(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})=\left\{D\in M(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}D+D^{*}=0\right\}.

Then we have the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

Any element
D\in\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
is uniquely expressed by

\displaystyle D=B+L(e_{2}E)+sE,\,\,B\in\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}),L\in\mathfrak{S}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}),s\in Ce_{1},

where
\mathfrak{S}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C})=\{L\in M(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C})\,|\,{}^{t}\!L=L\}
and
e_{1},e_{2}
are the basis in
\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C}:=\{1,e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}_{\rm span}
.

Proof..

First, we confirm that any element
D\in\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
is expressed by

\displaystyle D=\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}&d_{1}&d_{2}\\
-\overline{d}_{1}&b_{2}&d_{3}\\
-\overline{d}_{2}&-\overline{d}_{3}&b_{3}\end{pmatrix},\,\,b_{i}\in{\rm Im}(\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C}),d_{i}\in\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C}.

Here, set
b_{i}:=r_{i}e_{1}+c_{i}e_{2},d_{i}:=p_{i}+q_{i}e_{2},r_{i}\in C,c_{i},p_{i},q_{i}\in\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C},i=1,2,3
, then
D
is also expressed as follows:

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}r_{1}e_{1}&p_{1}&p_{2}\\
-\overline{p}_{1}&r_{2}e_{1}&p_{3}\\
-\overline{p}_{2}&-\overline{p}_{3}&r_{3}e_{1}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}e_{2}&q_{1}e_{2}&q_{2}e_{2}\\
q_{1}e_{2}&c_{2}e_{2}&q_{3}e_{2}\\
q_{2}e_{2}&q_{3}e_{2}&c_{3}e_{2}\end{pmatrix}\left(=:B^{\prime}+L(e_{2}E)\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}r_{1}e_{1}-s&p_{1}&p_{2}\\
-\overline{p}_{1}&r_{2}e_{1}-s&p_{3}\\
-\overline{p}_{2}&-\overline{p}_{3}&r_{3}e_{1}-s\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}&q_{1}&q_{2}\\
q_{1}&c_{2}&q_{3}\\
q_{2}&q_{3}&c_{3}\end{pmatrix}(e_{2}E)+sE,

where
s:=(1/3)(r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3})e_{1}\in Ce_{1}
is uniquely determined for
B^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{u}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C})
.

Hence we see that
D\in\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
is expressed by
D=B+L(e_{2}E)+sE,\,\,B\in\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}),L\in\mathfrak{S}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}),s\in Ce_{1}
. In order to prove the uniqueness of expression, it is sufficient to show that

\displaystyle D:=B^{\prime}+L(e_{2}E)=0,\,\,{\text{then}}\,\,B^{\prime}=0,L=0.

However, it is clear. With above, this lemma is proved.
∎

Subsequently, we will prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.2
.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C})
is isomorphic to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}(3,C)
:

\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C})\cong\mathfrak{sl}(3,C)
.

Proof..

We define a mapping
g:\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C})\to\mathfrak{sl}(3,C)
by

\displaystyle g(B)=\iota B-\overline{\iota}\,{}^{t}\!B,

where
\iota=(1/2)(1+ie_{1})
with
\iota^{2}=\iota,{\overline{\iota}}^{2}=\overline{\iota},\iota\overline{\iota}=0,\iota+\overline{\iota}=1
.

First, we will prove that
g
is well-defined. It follows from

\displaystyle\overline{g(B)}

\displaystyle=\overline{\iota B-\overline{\iota}\,{}^{t}\!B}=\overline{\iota}\overline{B}-\iota{}^{t}\overline{B}=\overline{\iota}(-{}^{t}\!B)-\iota(-B)=\iota B-\overline{\iota}\,{}^{t}\!B=g(B),

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm{tr}}(g(B))

\displaystyle=\iota\mbox{\rm{tr}}(B)-\overline{\iota}\mbox{\rm{tr}}({}^{t}\!B)=\iota\mbox{\rm{tr}}(B)-\overline{\iota}\mbox{\rm{tr}}(B)=0

that
g(B)\in\mathfrak{sl}(3,C)
. Hence
g
is well-defined. Subsequently, we will prove that
g
is a Lie-homomorphism. It follows that

\displaystyle[g(B_{1}),g(B_{2})]

\displaystyle=[\iota B_{1}-\overline{\iota}\,{}^{t}\!B_{1},\iota B_{2}-\overline{\iota}\,{}^{t}\!B_{2}]=\iota[B_{1},B_{2}]+\overline{\iota}[{}^{t}\!B_{1},{}^{t}\!B_{2}]

\displaystyle=\iota[B_{1},B_{2}]-\overline{\iota}\,{}^{t}\![B_{1},B_{2}]

\displaystyle=g([B_{1},B_{2}]).

Hence
g
is a Lie-homomorphism.

Next, we will prove that
g
is injective. In order to prove it, it is sufficient to show that
g(B)=0
implies
B=0
. From
g(B)=0
, that is,
\iota B=\overline{\iota}\,{}^{t}\!B
, apply
\iota
on both sides, then we have
\iota B=0
. Moreover, take transpose on both sides, we have
\iota{}^{t}\!B=\overline{\iota}B
and apply
\overline{\iota}
on this both sides, then we have
\overline{\iota}B=0
. Thus, since
\iota B=0
and
\overline{\iota}B=0
, we obtain
B=0
, so
g
is injective, and together with
\dim_{C}(\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}))=8=\dim_{C}(\mathfrak{sl}(3,C))
,
g
is surjective.

Therefore we have the required isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C})\cong\mathfrak{sl}(3,C).

∎

Now, we will prove the following theorem.

Theorem 5.3
.

The Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is isomorphic to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
:
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\cong\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
.

Proof..

Let the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C}):=\{B+L(e_{2}E)+sE\,|\,B\in\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}),L\in\mathfrak{S}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}),s\in Ce_{1}\}
(Lemma
5.1
).
Then we define a mapping
f_{7,C_{*}}:\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})\to(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
by

\displaystyle f_{7,C_{*}}(B+L(e_{2}E)+sE)=\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)),-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}),\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L},3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s),

where the mapping
g:\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C})\to\mathfrak{sl}(3,C)
is defined in the proof of Lemma
5.1
and
f_{6,C_{*}}
is defined in the proof of Corollary
4.6
.

First, we will prove that
f_{7,C_{*}}
is well-defined. From Corollary
4.6
and Lemma
5.2
, we have
f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B))\in(\mathfrak{e}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, and in addition, it is easy to verify
-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}),\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L}\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
and
3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s\in C
. Hence
f_{7,C_{*}}
is well-defined. Subsequently, we will prove that
f_{7,C_{*}}
is a Lie-homomorphism. It follows that

\displaystyle(1)

\displaystyle\quad[f_{7,C_{*}}(B),f_{7,C_{*}}(B^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=[\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)),0,0,0),\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B^{\prime})),0,0,0)]

\displaystyle=\varPhi([f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)),f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B^{\prime})],0,0,0)

\displaystyle=\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}([g(B),g(B^{\prime})]),0,0,0)

\displaystyle=\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}(g([B,B^{\prime}])),0,0,0)

\displaystyle=f_{7,C_{*}}([B,B^{\prime}]),

\displaystyle(2)

\displaystyle\quad[f_{7,C_{*}}(B),f_{7,C_{*}}(L(e_{2}E))]

\displaystyle=[\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)),0,0,0),\varPhi(0,-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}),\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L},0)]

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B))(-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L})),-{}^{t}\!(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)))(\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L}),0)\,\,(-{}^{t}\!(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)))=f_{6,C_{*}}(g(\overline{B}))

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,-(\iota(BL+L{}^{t}\!B)+\overline{\iota}(\overline{BL+L{}^{t}\!B})),\overline{\iota}(BL+L{}^{t}\!B)+\iota(\overline{BL+L{}^{t}\!B}),0)

\displaystyle=f_{7,C_{*}}((BL+L{}^{t}\!B)(e_{2}E))

\displaystyle=f_{7,C_{*}}([B,L(e_{2}E)]),

\displaystyle(3)

\displaystyle\quad[f_{7,C_{*}}(B),f_{7,C_{*}}(sE)]

\displaystyle=[\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)),0,0,0),\varPhi(0,0,0,3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s)]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=f_{7,C_{*}}([B,sE)]).

Here, in order to show
[f_{7,C_{*}}(L(e_{2}E),f_{7,C_{*}}(L^{\prime}(e_{2}E))]=f_{7,C_{*}}([L(e_{2}E),L^{\prime}(e_{2}E)])
as
(4)
, we use the following claims (i), (ii) and definition (iii):

\displaystyle({\rm i})\,\,[L(e_{2}E),L^{\prime}(e_{2}E))]=-L\overline{L^{\prime}}+L^{\prime}\overline{L},\,\,{\text{then}}\,\,-L\overline{L^{\prime}}+L^{\prime}\overline{L}-sE\in\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}),s:=(1/3)\mbox{\rm{tr}}(-L\overline{L^{\prime}}+L^{\prime}\overline{L}).

\displaystyle(\rm ii)\,\,\text{For $B:=-L\overline{L^{\prime}}+L^{\prime}\overline{L}-sE\in\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}),s:=(1/3)\mbox{\rm{tr}}(-L\overline{L^{\prime}}+L^{\prime}\overline{L}),g(B)$ is given by}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102ptg(B)=-\iota L\overline{L^{\prime}}+\iota L^{\prime}\overline{L}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}}L-\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}L^{\prime}-(\iota-\overline{\iota})sE,

\displaystyle\qquad\text{where the mapping $g$ is defined in the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma 5.2}}.

\displaystyle(\rm iii)\,\,\text{For $A,B\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}$, as that in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{iy0}{}{}, Definition (p.76)]}, an element $A\vee B\in(\mathfrak{e}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}$ is defined by }

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102ptA\vee B=[\tilde{A},\tilde{B}]+\left(A\circ B-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}(A,B)E\right)^{\sim},

where for
A\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, the
C
-linear transformation
\tilde{A}:(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\to(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is defined by
\tilde{A}X=A\circ X
.

Now, we begin the proof of
(4)
.

\displaystyle(4)

\displaystyle\quad[f_{7,C_{*}}(L(e_{2}E),f_{7,C_{*}}(L^{\prime}(e_{2}E))]

\displaystyle=[\varPhi(0,-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}),\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L},0),\varPhi(0,-(\iota L^{\prime}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}}),\overline{\iota}\,L^{\prime}+\iota\,\overline{L^{\prime}},0)]

\displaystyle=\varPhi(-2(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L})\vee(\overline{\iota}\,L^{\prime}+\iota\,\overline{L^{\prime}})+2(\iota L^{\prime}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}})\vee(\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L}),0,0,

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L},\overline{\iota}\,L^{\prime}+\iota\,\overline{L^{\prime}})+(\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L},\iota L^{\prime}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}}),

where using the formula of the definition
{\rm(iii)}
, the explicit form of the action to
(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
of
-2(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L})\vee(\overline{\iota}\,L^{\prime}+\iota\,\overline{L^{\prime}})+2(\iota L^{\prime}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}})\vee(\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L})
above is expressed by

\displaystyle\quad(-2(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L})\vee(\overline{\iota}\,L^{\prime}+\iota\,\overline{L^{\prime}})+2(\iota L^{\prime}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}})\vee(\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L}))X,\,\,X\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})

\displaystyle=(-\iota L\overline{L^{\prime}}+\iota L^{\prime}\overline{L}-\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}L^{\prime}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}}L)X+X(-\iota\overline{L^{\prime}}L+\iota\overline{L}L^{\prime}-\overline{\iota}\,L^{\prime}\overline{L}+\overline{\iota}\,L\overline{L^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\hskip 145.10905pt+((2/3)\mbox{\rm{tr}}(\iota L\overline{L^{\prime}}+\overline{\iota}\overline{L}L^{\prime})-(2/3)\mbox{\rm{tr}}(\iota L^{\prime}\overline{L}+\overline{\iota}\overline{L^{\prime}}L))X

\displaystyle=(-\iota L\overline{L^{\prime}}+\iota L^{\prime}\overline{L}-\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}L^{\prime}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}}L)X+X(-\iota\overline{L^{\prime}}L+\iota\overline{L}L^{\prime}-\overline{\iota}\,L^{\prime}\overline{L}+\overline{\iota}\,L\overline{L^{\prime}})-2(\iota-\overline{\iota})sX

\displaystyle=(-\iota L\overline{L^{\prime}}+\iota L^{\prime}\overline{L}-\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}L^{\prime}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}}L-(\iota-\overline{\iota})s)X+X(-\iota\overline{L^{\prime}}L+\iota\overline{L}L^{\prime}-\overline{\iota}\,L^{\prime}\overline{L}+\overline{\iota}\,L\overline{L^{\prime}}-(\iota-\overline{\iota})s)

\displaystyle=g(B)X+X\,{}^{t}\!g(B)\,\,(\text{claim (ii)})

\displaystyle=f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B))X,

note that in the calculations above, the calculations from the first line to second line are omitted because these are long,
and as for
-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L},\overline{\iota}\,L^{\prime}+\iota\,\overline{L^{\prime}})+(\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L},\iota L^{\prime}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}})
, using
(X,Y)=\mbox{\rm{tr}}(X\circ Y),X,Y\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, it follows that

\displaystyle-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L},\overline{\iota}\,L^{\prime}+\iota\,\overline{L^{\prime}})+(\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L},\iota L^{\prime}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=-\mbox{\rm{tr}}(\iota L\overline{L^{\prime}}+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}L^{\prime})+\mbox{\rm{tr}}(\overline{\iota}L\overline{L^{\prime}}+\iota\overline{L}L^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\mbox{\rm{tr}}((\iota-\overline{\iota})L\overline{L^{\prime}}-(\iota-\overline{\iota})\overline{L}L^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\iota-\overline{\iota})\mbox{\rm{tr}}(-L\overline{L^{\prime}}+\overline{L}L^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\iota-\overline{\iota})\mbox{\rm{tr}}(-L\overline{L^{\prime}}+L^{\prime}\overline{L})

\displaystyle=3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s.

Hence we have

\displaystyle[f_{7,C_{*}}(L(e_{2}E),f_{7,C_{*}}(L^{\prime}(e_{2}E))]=\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)),0,0,3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s).

On the other hand, it follows that

\displaystyle f_{7,C_{*}}([L(e_{2}E),L^{\prime}(e_{2}E)])

\displaystyle=f_{7,C_{*}}(-L\overline{L^{\prime}}+L^{\prime}\overline{L})

\displaystyle=f_{7,C_{*}}((-L\overline{L^{\prime}}+L^{\prime}\overline{L}-sE)+sE)\,\,(\text{claim (i)})

\displaystyle=\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)),0,0,3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s).

With above, we obtain

\displaystyle[f_{7,C_{*}}(L(e_{2}E),f_{7,C_{*}}(L^{\prime}(e_{2}E))]=f_{7,C_{*}}([L(e_{2}E,L^{\prime}(e_{2}E))]).

Further, the proof of a Lie-homomorphism is continued. It follows that

\displaystyle(5)

\displaystyle\quad[f_{7,C_{*}}(L(e_{2}E),f_{7,C_{*}}(sE))]

\displaystyle=[\varPhi(0,-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}),\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L},0),\varPhi(0,0,0,3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s)]

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,(-2/3)3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s(-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L})),(2/3)3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s(\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L}),0)

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,2(\iota sL-\overline{\iota}s\overline{L}),-2(\overline{\iota}sL-\iota s\overline{L}),0)

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,2(\iota sL+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{sL}),-2(\overline{\iota}sL+\iota\overline{sL}),0)

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,-(\iota(-2sL)+\overline{\iota}(\overline{-2sL})),\overline{\iota}(-2sL)+\iota(\overline{-2sL}),0)

\displaystyle=f_{7,C_{*}}((-2sL)(e_{2}E))

\displaystyle=f_{7,C_{*}}([L(e_{2}E),sE]),

\displaystyle(6)

\displaystyle\quad[f_{7,C_{*}}(sE),f_{7,C_{*}}(s^{\prime}E))]

\displaystyle=[\varPhi(0,0,0,3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s),\varPhi(0,0,0,3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=f_{7,C_{*}}([sE,s^{\prime}E]).

Consequently, the proof of homomorphism is completed.

Next, we will prove that
f_{7,C_{*}}
is injective. Since we easily see
\mbox{\rm{Ker}}\,f_{7,C_{*}}=\{0\}
, it is clear. Finally, we will prove that
f_{7,C_{*}}
is surjective. Since
f_{7,C_{*}}
is injective and together with
\dim(\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C}))=21=\dim_{C}((\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})
(in the beginning of this section (p.18)),
f_{7,C_{*}}
is surjective.

Therefore we have the required isomorphism

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\cong\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C}).

∎

We define a
C
-linear transformation
\lambda
of
(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
by

\displaystyle\lambda(X,Y,\xi,\eta)=(Y,-X,\eta,-\xi).

Then we have
\lambda\in E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}\subset(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
and
\lambda^{2}=-1,\lambda^{4}=1
. Hereafter, the element
\lambda
plays important role.

We define a space
(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
by

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}

\displaystyle=\left\{P\in(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}P\times P=0,P\not=0\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{P=(X,Y,\xi,\eta)\in(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\begin{array}[]{l}X\vee Y=0,X\times X=\eta Y,\\
Y\times Y=\xi X,(X,Y)=3\xi\eta,\\
P\not=0\end{array}\right\}.

Then we have the following proposition.

Proposition 5.4
.

For
\alpha\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, we have
\alpha(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}=(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

Proof..

We can prove this proposition as that in the proof of
[
8
, Proposition 4.2.2 (1)]
.
∎

Moreover,
\alpha\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
leaves the alternative inner product invariant:
\{\alpha P,\alpha Q\}=\{P,Q\},P,Q\in(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. This proof can be also proved as that in the proof of
[
8
, Proposition 4.2.2 (2)]
.

We consider the following subgroup
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\dot{1},\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
of
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
:

\displaystyle((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\dot{1},\text{\@text@daccent{$ 1 $}}}=\left\{\alpha\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\alpha\dot{1}=\dot{1},\alpha\text{\@text@daccent{$ 1 $}}=\text{\@text@daccent{$ 1 $}}\right\}.

Proposition 5.5
.

The group
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\dot{1},\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
is isomorphic to the group
(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
:

((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\dot{1},\underset{\dot{}}{1}}\cong(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

Proof..

We can prove this proposition as that in the proof of
[
4
, Proposition 4.4.1]
.
∎

Subsequently, we consider the following subgroup
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
of
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
:

\displaystyle((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\text{\@text@daccent{$ 1 $}}}=\left\{\alpha\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\alpha\underset{\dot{}}{1}=\text{\@text@daccent{$ 1 $}}\right\}.

Lemma 5.6
.

The Lie algebra
((\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
of the group
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
is given by

\displaystyle((\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\varPhi(\phi,A,B,\nu)\in(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\varPhi(\phi,A,B,\nu)\underset{\dot{}}{1}=0\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\varPhi(\phi,0,B,0)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\phi\in(\mathfrak{e}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},B\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\right\}.

In particular, we have
\dim_{C}(((\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}})=8+6=14
.

Proof..

Let
\varPhi(\phi,A,B,\nu)\in(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Then, since it follows that

\displaystyle\varPhi(\phi,A,B,\nu)\underset{\dot{}}{1}=(A,0,0,-\nu),

we have
A=0
and
\nu=0
. Thus we have the required result.
∎

Then we have the following proposition.

Proposition 5.7
.

The group
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
is the semi-direct product of groups
\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))
and
(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
:

((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}=\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))\rtimes(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, where the group
\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0)):=\{\exp(\varPhi(0,0,B,0))\,|\,\allowbreak B\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\}
.

In particular,the group
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
is connected.

Proof..

Let
\alpha\in((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
and set
\alpha\dot{1}:=(X,Y,\xi,\eta)
. Then it follows from

\displaystyle 1=\{\dot{1},\underset{\dot{}}{1}\}=\{\alpha\dot{1},\alpha\underset{\dot{}}{1}\}=\{(X,Y,\xi,\eta),(0,0,0,1)\}=\xi

that
\xi=1
, that is,
\alpha\dot{1}=(X,Y,1,\eta)
, and moreover
\alpha\dot{1}
is of form
(Y\times Y,Y,1,(1/3)(Y\times Y,Y))
from
\dot{1}\in(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
:

\displaystyle\alpha\dot{1}=(Y\times Y,Y,1,\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}(Y\times Y,Y)).

Here, set
\delta:=\varPhi(0,0,B,0)
. Then it follows that

\displaystyle\delta\dot{1}

\displaystyle=\exp(\varPhi(0,0,B,0))\dot{1}=\left(\displaystyle{\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}(\varPhi(0,0,B,0))^{n}}\right)\dot{1}=(B\times B,B,1,\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}(B\times B,B)),

and moreover it is easy to verify that
\delta\underset{\dot{}}{1}=\underset{\dot{}}{1}
.

Hence we have

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}\alpha\dot{1}

\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}(Y\times Y,Y,1,\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}(Y\times Y,Y))=\dot{1},

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}\alpha\underset{\dot{}}{1}

\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}\underset{\dot{}}{1}=\underset{\dot{}}{1}.

Hence, since we have
\delta^{-1}\alpha\in(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
from Proposition
5.5
, the group
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
is of the form

\displaystyle((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}=\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}.

Next, we will show that
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
is the semi-direct product of groups
\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))
and
(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. For
\beta\in(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, it is to verify that

\displaystyle\beta\exp(\varPhi(0,0,B,0))\beta^{-1}=\exp(\varPhi(0,0,{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}B,0)).

Indeed, for
(X,Y,\xi,\eta)\in(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, first it follows that

\displaystyle\beta\varPhi(0,0,B,0)\beta^{-1}(X,Y,\xi,\eta)

\displaystyle=\beta\varPhi(0,0,B,0)(\beta^{-1}X,{}^{t}\!\beta Y,\xi,\eta)

\displaystyle=\beta(2B\times{}^{t}\!\beta Y,\xi B,0,(B,\beta^{-1}X))

\displaystyle=(\beta(2B\times{}^{t}\!\beta Y),{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}\xi B,0,(B,\beta^{-1}X))

\displaystyle=(2{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}B\times Y,\xi\,{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}B,0,({}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}B,X))

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,0,{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}B,0)(X,Y,\xi,\eta),

that is,
\beta\varPhi(0,0,B,0)\beta^{-1}=\varPhi(0,0,{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}B,0)
. Using this formula, we have the following

\displaystyle\beta\exp(\varPhi(0,0,B,0))\beta^{-1}

\displaystyle=\beta\left(\displaystyle{\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}(\varPhi(0,0,B,0))^{n}}\right)\beta^{-1}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}(\beta\varPhi(0,0,B,0)\beta^{-1})^{n}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}(\varPhi(0,0,{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}B,0))^{n}}

\displaystyle=\exp(\varPhi(0,0,{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}B,0)).

Hence, for
\alpha\in((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
, it follows from
\alpha=\delta\beta\in\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}=((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
that

\displaystyle\alpha\exp(\varPhi(0,0,B,0))\alpha^{-1}

\displaystyle=\delta\beta\exp(\varPhi(0,0,B,0))\beta^{-1}\delta^{-1}

\displaystyle=\delta\exp(\varPhi(0,0,{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}B,0))\delta^{-1}\in\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0)).

Thus the group
\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))
is a normal subgroup of
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
.
In addition, we have a split exact sequence

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))\overset{j}{\longrightarrow}((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}\overset{\overset{p}{\scalebox{1.0}{$\longrightarrow$}}}{\underset{s}{\longleftarrow}}(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\longrightarrow 1.

Indeed, first we define a mapping
j
by
j(\delta)=\delta
. Then it is clear that
j
is a injective homomorphism. Subsequently, we define a mapping
p
by
p(\alpha):=p(\delta\beta)=\beta,\delta\in\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0)),\beta\in(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Then, since it follows that

\displaystyle p(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=p((\delta_{1}\beta_{1})(\delta_{2}\beta_{2}))=p(\delta_{1}(\beta_{1}\delta_{2}{\beta_{1}}^{-1})\beta_{1}\beta_{2})\,\,(\beta_{1}\delta_{2}{\beta_{1}}^{-1}=:{\delta_{2}}^{\prime}\in\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0)))

\displaystyle=p((\delta_{1}{\delta_{2}}^{\prime})(\beta_{1}\beta_{2}))=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=p(\alpha_{1})p(\alpha_{2}),

p
is a homomorphism. Let
\beta\in(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, then there exists
\alpha\in((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
such that
\alpha=\delta\beta
for
\delta\in\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))
. This implies that
p
is surjective. Finally, we define a mapping
s
by
s(\beta):=s(\delta^{-1}\alpha)=\alpha
. Then, as in the mapping
p
, we easily see that
s
is a homomorphism and
ps=1
. With above, the short sequence is a split exact sequence.

Thus the group
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
is the semi-direct product of groups
\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))
and
(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
:

\displaystyle((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}=\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))\rtimes(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}.

Finally, the connectedness of the group
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
follows from the connectedness of the groups
\exp(\varPhi(0,0,(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},0))
and
(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
are connected (Proposition
4.4
).
∎

Now, we will prove the connectedness of the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

Theorem 5.8
.

The homogeneous space
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}/((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
is homeomorphic to the space
(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
:

(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\allowbreak/((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}\simeq(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

In particular, the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is connected.

Proof..

Obviously, the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
acts on the space
(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, so we will prove that this action is transitive. In order to prove the transitivity, it is sufficient to show that any element
P\in(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
can be transformed to
\underset{\dot{}}{1}\in(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
by some
\alpha\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

Case (i) where
P=(X,Y,\xi,\eta),\eta\not=0
.

From the definition of
(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
,
P
is of the form

\displaystyle P=(X,\displaystyle\frac{1}{\eta}(X\times X),\displaystyle\frac{1}{3\eta}(X,X\times X),\eta).

Well, for
\varPhi(0,A,0,\nu)\in(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},\nu\not=0
, we will compute
\varPhi(0,A,0,\nu)^{n}\underset{\dot{}}{1}
:

\displaystyle\varPhi(0,A,0,\nu)^{n}\underset{\dot{}}{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\left(\displaystyle\frac{3}{\nu}\right)\left(\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\right)(-\nu)^{n}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(-\displaystyle\frac{\nu}{3}\right)^{n}\right)A\\[5.69054pt]
\left(\displaystyle\frac{3}{\nu}\right)^{2}\left(\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(-\displaystyle\frac{\nu}{3}\right)^{n}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}(-\nu)^{n}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\left(\displaystyle\frac{\nu}{3}\right)^{n}\right)(A\times A)\\[5.69054pt]
\left(\displaystyle\frac{3}{\nu}\right)^{3}\left(\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}\nu^{n}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}(-\nu)^{n}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{16}\left(-\displaystyle\frac{\nu}{3}\right)^{n}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{16}\left(\displaystyle\frac{\nu}{3}\right)^{n}\right)(A,A\times A)\\[5.69054pt]
(-\nu)^{n}\end{pmatrix}.

Then, using the formula above, we have the following result

\displaystyle(\exp(\varPhi(0,A,0,\nu))\underset{\dot{}}{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\displaystyle{\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}\varPhi(0,A,0,\nu)^{n}}\right)\underset{\dot{}}{1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\left(\displaystyle\frac{3}{\nu}\right)\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}e^{-\nu}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}e^{-\frac{\nu}{3}}\right)A\\[5.69054pt]
\left(\displaystyle\frac{3}{\nu}\right)^{2}\left(\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\right)e^{-\frac{\nu}{3}}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}e^{-\nu}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}e^{\frac{\nu}{3}}\right)(A\times A)\\[5.69054pt]
\left(\displaystyle\frac{3}{\nu}\right)^{3}\left(\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}e^{\nu}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}e^{-\nu}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{16}e^{-\frac{\nu}{3}}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{16}e^{\frac{\nu}{3}}\right)(A,A\times A)\\[5.69054pt]
e^{-\nu}\end{pmatrix}.

Hence, for a given
P=(X,(1/\eta)(X\times X),(1/3\eta)(X,X\times X),\eta)\in(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, we choose
\nu\in C
such that
\eta=e^{-\nu}
and construct
A:=((3/\nu)((1/2)e^{-\nu})+(1/2)e^{-\nu/3})^{-1}X\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
using
X
and
\nu
.
Then we have

\displaystyle(\exp(\varPhi(0,A,0,\nu))\underset{\dot{}}{1}=(X,\displaystyle\frac{1}{\eta}(X\times X),\displaystyle\frac{1}{3\eta}(X,X\times X),\eta).

Thus there exists
(\exp(\varPhi(0,A,0,\nu))\in((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{0}
such that
(\exp(\varPhi(0,A,0,\nu))^{-1}(X,(1/\eta)(X\times X),(1/3\eta)\allowbreak(X,X\times X),\eta)=\underset{\dot{}}{1}
.

For
\varPhi(0,A,0,\nu)\in(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, if
\nu=0
, we easily see

\displaystyle(\exp(\varPhi(0,A,0,0))\underset{\dot{}}{1}=(A,A\times A,\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}(A,A\times A),1).

Hence, for a given
P=(X,(1/\eta)(X\times X),(1/3\eta)(X,X\times X),\eta)\in(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, we choose
X
as
A
, then we have

\displaystyle(\exp(\varPhi(0,X,0,0))\underset{\dot{}}{1}=(X,\displaystyle\frac{1}{\eta}(X\times X),\displaystyle\frac{1}{3\eta}(X,X\times X),\eta).

Thus, as in the case above, there exists
(\exp(\varPhi(0,A,0,0))\in((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{0}
such that
(\exp(\varPhi(0,X,0,0))^{-1}(X,\allowbreak(1/\eta)(X\times X),(1/3\eta)(X,X\times X),\eta)=\underset{\dot{}}{1}
.

Case (ii) where
P=(X,Y,\xi,0),\xi\not=0
.

Let the element
\lambda\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
be the
C
-linear transformation of
(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Then, since we have
\lambda(X,Y,\xi,0)\allowbreak=(Y,-X,0,\xi),\xi\not=0
, this case is reduced to (i).

Case (iii) where
P=(X,Y,0,0),Y\not=0
.

Note that
Y
satisfies the condition
Y\times Y=0
.
For a given
P=(X,Y,0,0),Y\not=0
, we choose
\tau Y
, then we have

\displaystyle\exp(\varPhi(0,\tau Y,0,0))(X,Y,0,0)

\displaystyle=(X,Y+2\tau Y\times X,(\tau Y,Y)+(\tau Y,\tau Y\times X),0)

\displaystyle=(X,Y+2\tau Y\times X,(\tau Y,Y)+(X,\tau Y\times\tau Y),0)

\displaystyle=(X,Y+2\tau Y\times X,(\tau Y,Y)+(X,\tau(Y\times Y)),0)

\displaystyle=(X,Y+2\tau Y\times X,(\tau Y,Y),0).

Here, we confirm that
(\tau Y,Y)=0
if and only if
Y=0
, so that we see
(\tau Y,Y)\not=0
from
Y\not=0
. Hence this case is reduced to (ii).

Case (iv) where
P=(X,0,0,0),X\not=0
.

As in the case (ii), let the element
\lambda\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
again. Then, since we have
\lambda(X,0,0,0)=(0,-X,0,0),X\allowbreak\not=0
, this case is reduced to (iii).

With above, the proof of transitive is completed.

Thus it follows from the transitivity that
(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}=((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{0}\text{\@text@daccent{$ 1 $}}
, so that
(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is connected.
The group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
acts transitively on
(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
and the isotropy subgroup of
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
at
$̣1$
is
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
.

Therefore we have the homeomorphism

\displaystyle(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}/((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}\simeq(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}.

Finally, the connectedness of
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
follows from the connectedness of
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})_{\underset{\dot{}}{1}}
and
(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
(Proposition
5.7
).
∎

We will determine the center of the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Before that, for
\theta\in C^{*}:=\{\theta\in C\,|\,\theta\not=0\}
, we define a
C
-linear transformation
\phi(\theta)
of
(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
by

\displaystyle\phi(\theta)(X,Y,\xi,\eta)=(\theta^{-1}X,\theta Y,\theta^{3}\xi,\theta^{-3}\eta).

Then we have
\phi(\theta)\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

Theorem 5.9
.

The center
z((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})
of the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is the cyclic group of order two:

\displaystyle z((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})=\{1,-1\}\cong\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}_{2}.

Proof..

Let
\alpha\in z((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})
. From the commutativity with
\beta\in(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\subset(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, we have
\beta\alpha\dot{1}=\alpha\beta\dot{1}=\alpha\dot{1}
. Set
\alpha\dot{1}=(X,Y,\xi,\eta)\in(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, then from
(\beta X,{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}Y,\xi,\eta)=(X,Y,\xi,\eta)
, we have

\displaystyle\beta X=X,\;\;{}^{t}\!\beta^{-1}Y=Y\quad\text{for all}\quad\beta\in(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}.

Hence we have
X=Y=0
. Indeed, note that
z((E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})=z(SL(3,C))(=\{1,\omega 1,\omega^{2}1\})
follows from Theorem
4.5
. By letting
\omega 1\in z((E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}),\omega\in C,\omega^{3}=1,\omega\not=1
as
\beta
, we easily obtain
X=Y=0
.
Thus
\alpha\dot{1}
is of the form

\displaystyle\alpha\dot{1}=(0,0,\xi,\eta).

Since
\alpha\dot{1}\in(\mathfrak{M}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, we have
\xi\eta=0
, that is,
\xi=0
or
\eta=0
. Here, suppose
\xi=0
, then we have
\alpha\dot{1}=(0,0,0,\eta),\eta\neq 0
. Also from the commutativity with
\phi(\theta)\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, we have

\displaystyle(0,0,0,\theta^{-3}\eta)

\displaystyle=\phi(\theta)(0,0,0,\eta)=\phi(\theta)\alpha\dot{1}=\alpha\phi(\theta)\dot{1}

\displaystyle=\alpha(0,0,\theta^{3},0)=\theta^{3}\alpha\dot{1}

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,\theta^{3}\eta),

that is,
\theta^{-3}\eta=\theta^{3}\eta
for all
\theta\in C^{*}
. This is a contradiction. Hence, since we see
\xi\neq 0
, we have
\eta=0
, that is,
\alpha\dot{1}=(0,0,\xi,0)
.

Subsequently, set
\alpha\text{\@text@daccent{$ 1 $}}=(Z,W,\rho,\zeta)
, then by an argument similar to that in the case where
\alpha\dot{1}
above, we have
\alpha\underset{\dot{}}{1}=(0,0,0,\zeta)
. In addition, from
\{\alpha\dot{1},\alpha\underset{\dot{}}{1}\}=1
, we have
\xi\zeta=1
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\alpha\dot{1}=(0,0,\xi,0),\quad\alpha\underset{\dot{}}{1}=(0,0,0,\xi^{-1}).

Moreover, from the commutativity with
\lambda\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, we have

\displaystyle(0,0,0,-\xi)

\displaystyle=\lambda(0,0,\xi,0)=\lambda\alpha\dot{1}=\alpha\lambda\dot{1}

\displaystyle=\alpha(0,0,0,-1)

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,-\xi^{-1}),

that is,
\xi=\xi^{-1}
. Hence we have
\xi=1
or
\xi=-1
.

In the case where
\xi=1
. From
\alpha\dot{1}=\dot{1}
and
\alpha\underset{\dot{}}{1}=\underset{\dot{}}{1}
, we have
\alpha\in(E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
(Proposition
5.5
), so that we see
\alpha\in z((E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})=\{1,\omega 1,\omega^{2}1\}
as a necessary condition. Then
\alpha
is expressed by

\displaystyle\alpha=\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{\prime}1&0&0&0\cr 0&{\omega^{\prime}}^{-1}1&0&0\cr 0&0&1&0\cr 0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\,\,\omega^{\prime}:=1,\omega\;\text{or}\;\omega^{2}

as the element of
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.
Again from the commutativity with
\lambda
, we have

\displaystyle(0,\omega^{\prime}X,0,0)

\displaystyle=-\lambda(\omega^{\prime}X,0,0,0)=-\lambda\alpha(X,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=-\alpha\lambda(X,0,0,0)=\alpha(0,X,0,0)

\displaystyle=(0,{\omega^{\prime}}^{-1}X,0,0),

for all
X\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, that is,
\omega^{\prime}={\omega^{\prime}}^{-1}
. This implies
\omega^{\prime}=1
. Hence we have
\alpha=1
.

In the case where
\xi=-1
, we have
-\alpha\in z((E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})
, so that we have
-\alpha=1
by the similar argument as above.

With above, we obtain
z((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})\subset\{1,-1\}
, and vice versa.

Therefore we have the required result

\displaystyle z((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})=\{1,-1\}\cong\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}_{2}

∎

Now, we will determine the structure of the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

Theorem 5.10
.

The group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is isomorphic to the group
Sp(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
:

(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\cong Sp(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
.

Proof..

Since the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is connected (Theorem
5.8
) and its associated Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is isomorphic to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
(Theorem
5.3
), the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is isomorphic to either of the following groups

\displaystyle Sp(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C}),\quad Sp(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})/\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}_{2}.

Therefore, from Theorem
5.9
,
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
have to be isomorphic to
Sp(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
:

\displaystyle(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\cong Sp(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C}).

∎

Now, in order to determine the structure of the group
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
, we make some preparations below.

First, we consider the following subgroup
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}
of
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
:

\displaystyle((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}:=\left\{\alpha\in(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\tau\lambda\alpha\lambda^{-1}\tau=\alpha\right\}.

Then we have the following proposition.

Proposition 5.11
.

The group
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}
coincides with the group
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
:

((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}=E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
.

Proof..

Let
\alpha\in((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}
. It follows from
\langle P,Q\rangle=\{\tau\lambda P,Q\},P,Q\in(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
that

\displaystyle\langle\alpha P,\alpha Q\rangle=\{\tau\lambda\alpha P,\alpha Q\}=\{\alpha\tau\lambda P,\alpha Q\}=\{\tau\lambda P,Q\}=\langle P,Q\rangle.

Hence we have
\alpha\in E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
.

Conversely, let
\beta\in E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
. It follows from
(P,Q)=\{\lambda P,Q\}
that

\displaystyle\langle\beta P,\beta Q\rangle

\displaystyle=\{\tau\lambda\beta P,\beta Q\}=\{\lambda\tau\beta P,\beta Q\}=(\tau\beta P,\beta Q),

\displaystyle\langle P,Q\rangle

\displaystyle=\{\tau\lambda P,Q\}=\{\beta\tau\lambda P,\beta Q\}=\{\lambda\lambda^{-1}\beta\tau\lambda P,\beta Q\}=(\lambda^{-1}\beta\tau\lambda P,\beta Q).

Hence we have
\tau\beta=\lambda^{-1}\beta\tau\lambda
, that is,
\tau\lambda\beta=\beta\tau\lambda
, so
\beta\in((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}
.

With above, this proposition is proved.
∎

Hence the following corollary follows from Proposition
5.11
above.

Corollary 5.12
.

The Lie algebra
((\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}
of the group
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}
coincides with the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
of the group
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
:
((\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}=\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
.

Proof..

Since the group
Sp(3,C)
is simply connected (
[
10
, Theorem 6.13]
) and together with
Sp(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})\cong Sp(3,C)
(
[
10
, Theorem 3.6 (2)]
),
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is simply connected (Theorem
5.10
). Hence
((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}
is connected, so that
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
is also connected (Proposition
5.11
). Thus we have the required result.
∎

We consider the following subgroup
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})_{\dot{1}}
of
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
:

\displaystyle(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})_{\dot{1}}=\left\{\alpha\in E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\alpha\dot{1}=\dot{1}\right\}.

Proposition 5.13
.

The group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})_{\dot{1}}
is isomorphic to the group
E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
:

(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})_{\dot{1}}\cong E_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
.

Proof..

We can prove this proposition as that in the proof of
[
8
, Theorem 4.7.2]
.
∎

As for the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sp}(3)
, we have the following lemma as that in Lemma
5.1
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Any element
D\in\mathfrak{sp}(3)
is uniquely expressed by

\displaystyle D=B+L(e_{2}E)+sE,\,\,B\in\mathfrak{su}(3),L\in\mathfrak{S}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}),s\in\mbox{\boldmath$R$}e_{1},

where
\mathfrak{S}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$})=\{L\in M(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$})\,|\,{}^{t}\!L=L\}
and
e_{1},e_{2}
are the basis in
\mbox{\boldmath$H$}:=\{1,e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}_{\rm span}
.

Proof..

We can prove this lemma by replacing
\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C},\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}
with
\mbox{\boldmath$H$},\mbox{\boldmath$C$}
in the proof of Lemma
5.1
, respectively.
∎

We will prove the following theorem.

Theorem 5.15
.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
is isomorphic to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sp}(3)
:

\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}\cong\mathfrak{sp}(3)
.

Proof..

Let the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sp}(3):=\{B+L(e_{2}E)+sE\,|\,B\in\mathfrak{su}(3),L\in\mathfrak{S}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}),s\in\mbox{\boldmath$R$}e_{1}\}
(Lemma
5.14
) and the Lie algebra
((\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}
as
\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
.
Then we define a mapping
f_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}_{*}}:\mathfrak{sp}(3)\to(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{\tau\lambda}
by

\displaystyle f_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}_{*}}(B+L(e_{2}E)+sE)=\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)),-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}),\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L},3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s).

Note that this mapping is the restriction of the mapping
f_{7,C_{*}}
which is defined in the proof of Theorem
5.3
and moreover
g(B)\in\mathfrak{su}(3)\subset\mathfrak{sl}(3,C)
.

First, we will prove that
f_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}_{*}}
is well-defined. From Theorem
4.9
, we have
f_{6_{*}}(B)\in\mathfrak{e}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
, and in addition, it is easy to verify
-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}),\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L}\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},\overline{\iota}\,L+\iota\,\overline{L}=-\tau(-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\,\overline{L}))
and
3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s\in\mbox{\boldmath$R$}
. Hence
f_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}_{*}}
is well-defined. Subsequently, we will prove that
f_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}_{*}}
is a Lie-homomorphism.
However, since
f_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}_{*}}
is the restriction of the mapping
f_{7,C_{*}}
, we can prove this by same argument as that in the proof of Theorem
5.3
.

Next, we will prove that
f_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}_{*}}
is injective. Since we easily see
\mbox{\rm{Ker}}\,f_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}_{*}}=\mbox{\rm{Ker}}\,f_{7,C_{*}}=\{0\}
, it is clear.

Finally, we will prove that
f_{7,C_{*}}
is surjective. Let
\varPhi\in((\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}\subset(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Then there exists
B+L(e_{2}E)+sE\in\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C}),B\in\mathfrak{su}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}),L\in\mathfrak{S}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}^{C}),s\in Ce_{1}
such that
\varPhi=f_{7,C_{*}}(B+L(e_{2}E)+sE)
(Theorem
5.3
). Moreover,
\varPhi
satisfies the condition
(\tau\lambda)\varPhi(\lambda^{-1}\tau)=\varPhi
, that is,
(\tau\lambda)f_{7,C_{*}}(B+L(e_{2}E)+sE)(\lambda^{-1}\tau)=f_{7,C_{*}}(B+L(e_{2}E)+sE)
, so it follows that

\displaystyle(\tau\lambda)f_{7,C_{*}}(B+L(e_{2}E)+sE)(\lambda^{-1}\tau)

\displaystyle=(\tau\lambda)\varPhi(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)),-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\overline{L}),\overline{\iota}L+\iota\overline{L},3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s)(\lambda^{-1}\tau)

\displaystyle=\varPhi(-\tau\,{}^{t}(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)))\tau,-\tau(\overline{\iota}L+\iota\overline{L}),\tau(-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\overline{L})),3\tau(\iota-\overline{\iota})s).

Here, we use the following claim:

\displaystyle-\tau\,{}^{t}(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)))\tau=f_{6,C_{*}}(g(\tau B)).

Indeed, first it follows from Lemma
5.2
that

\displaystyle({}^{t}f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B))X,Y)

\displaystyle=(X,f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B))Y)=(X,g(B)Y+Y\,{}^{t}(g(B)))

\displaystyle=(X,g(B)Y)+(X,Y\,{}^{t}(g(B)))=({}^{t}(g(B))X,Y)+(g(B)X,Y)

\displaystyle=({}^{t}(g(B))X+g(B)X,Y)=(f_{6,C_{*}}({}^{t}(g(B)))X,Y),\;\;X,Y\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},

that is,
{}^{t}f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B))=f_{6,C_{*}}({}^{t}(g(B)))
, and moreover it follows that

\displaystyle\tau f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B))\tau X

\displaystyle=\tau(g(B)(\tau X)+(\tau X){}^{t}(g(B)))=(\tau g(B))X+X(\tau{}^{t}(g(B)))

\displaystyle=(\tau g(B))X+X{}^{t}(\tau g(B))=f_{6,C_{*}}(\tau g(B))X,\;\;X\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},

that is,
\tau f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B))\tau=f_{6,C_{*}}(\tau g(B))
. In addition, it is easy to verify that
-\tau\,{}^{t}(g(B))=g(\tau B)
. With above, the claim is shown. Hence
B\in\mathfrak{su}(3)
follows from
-\tau\,{}^{t}(f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B)))\tau=f_{6,C_{*}}(g(B))
.

As for the remaining parts,
L\in\mathfrak{S}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$})
follows from
-\tau(\overline{\iota}L+\iota\overline{L})=\overline{\iota}L+\iota\overline{L}
and
\tau(-(\iota L+\overline{\iota}\overline{L}))=\overline{\iota}L+\iota\overline{L}
and we have
s\in\mbox{\boldmath$R$}e_{1}
from
3\tau(\iota-\overline{\iota})s=3(\iota-\overline{\iota})s
. Thus there exists
B+L(e_{2}E)+sE\in\mathfrak{sp}(3),B\in\mathfrak{su}(3),L\in\mathfrak{S}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$C$}),s\in\mbox{\rm{Re}}_{1}
such that
\varPhi=f_{7,C_{*}}(B+L(e_{2}E)+sE)
. The proof of surjective is completed.

Therefore we have the required isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}\cong\mathfrak{sp}(3).

∎

Now, we will determine the structure of the group
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
.

Theorem 5.16
.

The group
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
is isomorphic to the group
Sp(3)
:

E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}\cong Sp(3)
.

Proof..

First, it is to verify that
z((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})\subset z(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})
, where both are the center of the groups
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
and
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
, respectively. Indeed, let
\delta\in z((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})
. Then, since
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}\subset(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
,
\delta
commutes with any elements of
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
. Hence we have
\delta\in z(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})
, that is,
z((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})\subset z(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})
. Here, since
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\cong Sp(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
(Theorem
5.10
) and
Sp(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
is simply connected (
[
10
, Theorems 3.6 (2), 6.13]
), the group
(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is also simply connected. Hence the group
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}=((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})^{\tau\lambda}
is connected (Proposition
5.11
). In addition, since the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
of its group is isomorphic to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sp}(3)
(Theorem
5.15
), the group
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
is isomorphic to either of the following groups

\displaystyle Sp(3),\quad Sp(3)/\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}_{2}.

In the latter case, if
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}\cong Sp(3/\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}_{2})
, then the center
z(Sp(3)/\mbox{\boldmath$Z$}_{2})
is trivial, so that the center
z(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})
is also trivial. However, since
z((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})\subset z(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})
as mentioned above and
z((E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C})=\{1,-1\}
(Theorem
5.9
), the elements
1,-1
at least belong to
z(E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})
. Thus this case is impossible.

Therefore
E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
has to be isomorphic to
Sp(3)
:

\displaystyle E_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}\cong Sp(3).

∎

We move the determination of the root system and the Dynkin diagram of the Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

We define a Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{7}=\left\{\varPhi_{7}=\varPhi(\phi,0,0,\nu)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\begin{array}[]{l}\phi=\tilde{T}_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}_{6},\\
\quad T_{0}=\tau_{1}E_{1}+\tau_{2}E_{2}+\tau_{3}E_{3}\in(({\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{C}$}}})^{C})_{0},\\
\qquad\quad\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}+\tau_{3}=0,\tau_{i}\in C,\\
\nu\in C\end{array}\right\}.

Then the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{7}
is a Cartan subalgebra of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

Theorem 5.17
.

The roots
\varDelta
of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
relative to
\mathfrak{h}_{7}
are given by

\displaystyle\varDelta=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\pm\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{3}),\pm\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{3}-\tau_{1}),\pm\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})\\[5.69054pt]
\pm(\tau_{1}+\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\pm(\tau_{2}+\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\pm(\tau_{3}+\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\\[5.69054pt]
\pm(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{1}-\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\pm(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{2}-\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\pm(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{3}-\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu)\end{array}\right\}.

Proof..

The roots of
(\mathfrak{e}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
are also the roots of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Indeed, let the root
\alpha
of
(\mathfrak{e}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
and its associated root vector
S\in(\mathfrak{e}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\subset(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Then we have

\displaystyle[\varPhi_{7},S]

\displaystyle=[\varPhi(\phi,0,0,\nu),\varPhi(S,0,0,0)]

\displaystyle=\varPhi([\phi,S],0,0,0)

\displaystyle=\varPhi(\alpha(\phi)S,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=\alpha(\phi)\varPhi(S,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=\alpha(\varPhi)S.

We will determine the remainders of roots. We will show a few examples.
First, let
\varPhi_{7}=\varPhi(\phi,0,\allowbreak 0,\nu)\in\mathfrak{h}_{7}
and
\varPhi(0,E_{1},0,0)\in(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Then it follows that

\displaystyle[\varPhi_{7},\varPhi(0,E_{1},0,0)]

\displaystyle=[\varPhi(\phi,0,0,\nu),\varPhi(0,E_{1},0,0)]

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,(\phi+(2/3)\nu)E_{1},0,0)

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,(\tau_{1}+(2/3)\nu)E_{1},0,0)

\displaystyle=(\tau_{1}+(2/3)\nu)\varPhi(0,E_{1},0,0),

that is,
[\varPhi_{7},\varPhi(0,E_{1},0,0)]=(\tau_{1}+(2/3)\nu)\varPhi(0,E_{1},0,0)
. Hence we see that
\tau_{1}+(2/3)\nu
is a root and
\varPhi(0,E_{1},0,0)
is an associated root vector. Next, let
\varPhi(0,F_{1}(1),0,0)\in(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Then it follows that

\displaystyle[\varPhi_{7},\varPhi(0,F_{1}(1),0,0)]

\displaystyle=[\varPhi(\phi,0,0,\nu),\varPhi(0,F_{1}(1),0,0)]

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,(\phi+(2/3)\nu)F_{1}(1),0,0)

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,\phi F_{1}(1)+(2/3)\nu F_{1}(1),0,0)

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,(1/2)(\tau_{2}+\tau_{3})F_{1}(1)+(2/3)\nu F_{1}(1),0,0)

\displaystyle=((1/2)(\tau_{2}+\tau_{3})+(2/3)\nu)\varPhi(0,F_{1}(1),0,0),

that is,
[\varPhi_{7},\varPhi(0,F_{1}(1),0,0)]=(-(1/2)\tau_{1}+(2/3)\nu)\varPhi(0,F_{1}(1),0,0)
. Hence we see that
-(1/2)\tau_{1}+(2/3)\nu
is a root and
\varPhi(0,F_{1}(1),0,0)
is an associated root vector. The remainders of roots and these associated root vectors except ones of the Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{e}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
and above are obtained as follows:

roots

associated root vectors

\tau_{2}+(2/3)\nu

\varPhi(0,E_{2},0,0)

\tau_{3}+(2/3)\nu

\varPhi(0,E_{3},0,0)

-(\tau_{1}+(2/3)\nu)

\varPhi(0,0,E_{1},0)

-(\tau_{2}+(2/3)\nu)

\varPhi(0,0,E_{2},0)

-(\tau_{3}+(2/3)\nu)

\varPhi(0,0,E_{3},0)

(1/2)\tau_{1}-(2/3)\nu

\varPhi(0,0,F_{1}(1),0)

-(1/2)\tau_{2}+(2/3)\nu

\varPhi(0,F_{2}(1),0,0)

(1/2)\tau_{2}-(2/3)\nu

\varPhi(0,0,F_{2}(1),0)

(-1/2)\tau_{3}+(2/3)\nu

\varPhi(0,F_{3}(1),0,0)

(1/2)\tau_{3}-(2/3)\nu

\varPhi(0,0,F_{3}(1),0)

Thus, since
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is spanned by
\mathfrak{h}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
and associated root vectors above, the roots obtained above are all.
∎

In
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, we define an inner product
(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})_{7}
by

\displaystyle(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})_{7}:=-2(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})_{6}-4(A_{1},B_{2})-4(A_{2},B_{1})-\displaystyle\frac{8}{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}.

Here, we will determine the Killing form of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

Theorem 5.18
.

The killing form
B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is given by

\displaystyle B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})

\displaystyle=-2(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})_{7}

\displaystyle=-4(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})_{6}+8(A_{1},B_{2})+8(A_{2},B_{1})+\displaystyle\frac{16}{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{8}{3}B_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})+8(A_{1},B_{2})+8(A_{2},B_{1})+\displaystyle\frac{16}{3}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{8}{5}\mbox{\rm{tr}}(\varPhi_{1}\varPhi_{2}).

Proof..

The Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is simple because of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\cong\mathfrak{sp}(3,\mbox{\boldmath$H$}^{C})
(Corollary
4.6
). By the analogues argument as that in
{\mathfrak{e}_{7}}^{C}
, we will determine the values
k,k^{\prime}\in C

such that

\displaystyle B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})=k(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})_{7}=k^{\prime}\mbox{\rm{tr}}(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2}).

First, in order to determine
k
, let
\varPhi_{1}=\varPhi_{2}=\varPhi(0,0,0,1)=:\varPhi_{0}
. Then we have

\displaystyle(\varPhi_{0},\varPhi_{0})_{7}=-\displaystyle\frac{8}{3}.

On the other hand, if follows from

\displaystyle(\mbox{\rm{ad}}\varPhi_{0})(\mbox{\rm{ad}}\varPhi_{0})\varPhi(\phi,A,B,\nu)

\displaystyle=[\varPhi_{0},[\varPhi_{0},\varPhi(\phi,A,B,\nu)]]

\displaystyle=[\varPhi_{0},\varPhi(0,\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}A,-\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}B,0)]

\displaystyle=\varPhi(0,\displaystyle\frac{4}{9}A,\displaystyle\frac{4}{9}B,0).

that
B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\mbox{\rm{ad}}\varPhi_{0},\mbox{\rm{ad}}\varPhi_{0})=\mbox{\rm{tr}}((\mbox{\rm{ad}}\varPhi_{0})(\mbox{\rm{ad}}\varPhi_{0}))=(4/9)\times 6\times 2=16/3
. Hence we have
k=-2
.

Next, we will determine
k^{\prime}
. Similarly, it follows from

\displaystyle\varPhi_{0}\varPhi_{0}(X,Y,\xi,\eta)=\varPhi_{0}(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}X,\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}Y,\xi,-\eta)=(\displaystyle\frac{1}{9}X,\displaystyle\frac{1}{9}Y,\xi,\eta)

that
\mbox{\rm{tr}}(\varPhi_{0}\varPhi_{0})=(1/9)\times 6\times 2+1+1=10/3
. Hence we have
k^{\prime}=8/5
.
∎

Subsequently, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 5.19
.

In the root system
\varDelta
of Theorem
5.17
,

\displaystyle\varPi=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}

is a fundamental root system of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, where

\alpha_{1}=(1/2)(\tau_{3}-\tau_{1}),\alpha_{2}=(-1/2)\tau_{3}+(2/3)\nu,\alpha_{3}=-(\tau_{2}+(2/3)\nu)
.
The Dynkin diagram of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is given by

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

Proof..

The remaining positive roots are expressed by
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
as follows:

\displaystyle(-1/2)(\tau_{2}-\tau_{3})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3},

\displaystyle(1/2)(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3},

\displaystyle\tau_{1}+(2/3)\nu

\displaystyle=2\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3},

\displaystyle\tau_{3}+(2/3)\nu

\displaystyle=2\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3},

\displaystyle(-1/2)\tau_{1}+(2/3)\nu

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},

\displaystyle(-1/2)\tau_{2}+(2/3)\nu

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}.

Hence we see that
\varPi=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}
is a fundamental root system of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Let the real part
\mathfrak{h}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
of
\mathfrak{h}_{7}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}=\left\{\varPhi=\varPhi(\phi,0,0,\nu)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\begin{array}[]{l}\phi=\tilde{T}_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}},\\
\quad T_{0}=\tau_{1}E_{1}+\tau_{2}E_{2}+\tau_{3}E_{3}\in(\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})_{0},\\
\qquad\quad\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}+\tau_{3}=0,\tau_{i}\in\mbox{\boldmath$R$},\\
\nu\in\mbox{\boldmath$R$}\end{array}\right\}.

Here, in
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, the Killing form
B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is given by

\displaystyle B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi,\varPhi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(8/3)B_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\phi,\phi^{\prime})+8(A,B^{\prime})+8(A^{\prime},B)+\displaystyle\frac{16}{3}\nu\nu^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(8/5)\mbox{\rm{tr}}\,(\varPhi\varPhi^{\prime}),

so is on
\mathfrak{h}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
. Hence, for
\varPhi=\varPhi(\phi,0,0,\nu),\varPhi^{\prime}=\varPhi(\phi^{\prime},0,0,\nu^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{h}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
, we easily obtain

\displaystyle B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi,\varPhi^{\prime})=4(\tau_{1}{\tau_{1}}^{\prime}+\tau_{2}{\tau_{2}}^{\prime}+\tau_{3}{\tau_{3}}^{\prime})+\displaystyle\frac{16}{3}\nu\nu^{\prime}.

Indeed, it follows from
B_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\phi,\phi^{\prime})=(3/2)(\tau_{1}{\tau_{1}}^{\prime}+\tau_{2}{\tau_{2}}^{\prime}+\tau_{3}{\tau_{3}}^{\prime})
that

\displaystyle B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi,\varPhi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{8}{3}B_{6,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\phi,\phi^{\prime})+\displaystyle\frac{16}{3}\nu\nu^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{8}{3}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}(\tau_{1}{\tau_{1}}^{\prime}+\tau_{2}{\tau_{2}}^{\prime}+\tau_{3}{\tau_{3}}^{\prime})+\displaystyle\frac{16}{3}\nu\nu^{\prime}

\displaystyle=4(\tau_{1}{\tau_{1}}^{\prime}+\tau_{2}{\tau_{2}}^{\prime}+\tau_{3}{\tau_{3}}^{\prime})+\displaystyle\frac{16}{3}\nu\nu^{\prime}.

Now, the canonical elements
\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}},\varPhi_{\alpha_{2}},\varPhi_{\alpha_{3}}\in\mathfrak{h}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
corresponding to
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}
are determined as follows:

\displaystyle\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\varPhi((-\tilde{E}_{1}+\tilde{E}_{3},0,0,0),

\displaystyle\varPhi_{\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}\varPhi(\tilde{E}_{1}+\tilde{E}_{2}-2\tilde{E}_{3},0,0,3)

\displaystyle\varPhi_{\alpha_{3}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}\varPhi(2\tilde{E}_{1}-4\tilde{E}_{2}+2\tilde{E}_{3},0,0,-3).

Indeed, let
\varPhi=\varPhi(\phi^{\prime},0,0,\nu^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{h}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
and set
\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}}=\varPhi(\phi,0,0,\nu)
, then it follows from
B_{7}(\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}},\varPhi)=\alpha_{1}(\varPhi)
that

\displaystyle B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}},\varPhi)

\displaystyle=4(\tau_{1}{\tau_{1}}^{\prime}+\tau_{2}{\tau_{2}}^{\prime}+\tau_{3}{\tau_{3}}^{\prime})+\displaystyle\frac{16}{3}\nu\nu^{\prime}

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(\varPhi)

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\tau_{1}}^{\prime}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\tau_{3}}^{\prime}

Hence we have

\displaystyle\tau_{1}=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{8},\tau_{2}=0,\tau_{3}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{8},\nu=0,

that is,
\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}}=(1/8)\varPhi((-\tilde{E}_{1}+\tilde{E}_{3},0,0,0)
. Note that
\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}}
can be also obtained by

\displaystyle\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}}=(B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\tilde{A}_{2}(1)+\tilde{F}_{2}(1),\tilde{A}_{2}(1)+\tilde{F}_{2}(1)))^{-1}[\tilde{A}_{2}(1)+\tilde{F}_{2}(1),\tilde{A}_{2}(1)+\tilde{F}_{2}(1))],

where the elements
\tilde{A}_{2}(1)+\tilde{F}_{2}(1),\tilde{A}_{2}(1)-\tilde{F}_{2}(1)
are the root vectors associated with the roots
\alpha_{1},-\alpha_{1}
.
For the remainders of
\varPhi_{\alpha_{i}},i=2,3
, as in the case above, we obtain the required results.

With above, we see that

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1})

\displaystyle=B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}},\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}})=4\cdot\left(\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\right)\cdot\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\right)+\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\right)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{8},

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}},\varPhi_{\alpha_{2}})=4\cdot\left(\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\right)\cdot\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\cdot\left(-\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}\right)\right)=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{16},

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3})

\displaystyle=B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi_{\alpha_{1}},\varPhi_{\alpha_{3}})=4\cdot\left(\left(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\right)\cdot\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}\right)=0,

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi_{\alpha_{2}},\varPhi_{\alpha_{2}})=4\cdot\left(\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}+\cdot\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}+\left(-\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}\right)\cdot\left(-\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}\right)\right)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{8},

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})

\displaystyle=B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi_{\alpha_{2}},\varPhi_{\alpha_{3}})=4\cdot\left(\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{24}\left(-\displaystyle\frac{4}{24}\right)+\left(-\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}\right)\cdot\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}\right)=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{8},

\displaystyle(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{3})

\displaystyle=B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi_{\alpha_{3}},\varPhi_{\alpha_{3}})=4\cdot\left(\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}+\left(-\displaystyle\frac{4}{24}\right)\cdot\left(-\displaystyle\frac{4}{24}\right)+\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{2}{24}\right)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}.

Hence, since we have

\displaystyle\cos\theta_{12}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}{\sqrt{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1})(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})}}=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2},\quad\cos\theta_{13}=\displaystyle\frac{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3})}{\sqrt{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1})(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{3})}}=0,

\displaystyle\cos\theta_{23}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})}{\sqrt{(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{3})}}=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\quad(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{3})=\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}>\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}=(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}),

we can draw the Dynkin diagram.
∎

6.
The root system, the Dynkin diagram of the Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}})^{C}
of the group
(E_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
and the Lie groups
(E_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},E_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}

We consider the following complex Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
which is given by replacing
\mathfrak{C}
with
R
in the complex Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{e}_{8}}^{C}
:

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\oplus(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\oplus(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\oplus C\oplus C\oplus C

\displaystyle=\left\{R=(\varPhi,P,Q,r,s,t)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\begin{array}[]{l}\varPhi\in(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},P,Q\in(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},\\
r,s,t\in C\end{array}\right\}

In particular, we have
\dim_{C}((\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{C}$}})^{C})=21+14\times 2+3=52
. In
(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, we can define a Lie bracket and can prove its simplicity as that in
{\mathfrak{e}_{8}}^{C}
.
In addition, we define a symmetric inner product
(R_{1},R_{2})_{8}
by

\displaystyle(R_{1},R_{2})_{8}:=(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})_{7}-\{Q_{1},P_{2}\}+\{P_{1},Q_{2}\}-8r_{1}r_{2}-4t_{1}s_{2}-4s_{1}t_{2},

where
R_{i}:=(\varPhi_{i},P_{i},Q_{i},r_{i},s_{i},t_{i})\in(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C},i=1,2
. Then
(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
leaves the symmetric inner product
(R_{1},R_{2})_{8}
invariant (cf.
[
8
, Lemma 5.3.1]
).

Then we have the following theorem.

Theorem 6.1
.

The Killing form
B_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}
of
(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is given by

\displaystyle B_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(R_{1},R_{2})

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\frac{9}{2}(R_{1},R_{2})_{8}

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\frac{9}{2}(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})_{7}+\displaystyle\frac{9}{2}\{Q_{1},P_{2}\}-\displaystyle\frac{9}{2}\{P_{1},Q_{2}\}+36r_{1}r_{2}+18t_{1}s_{2}+18s_{1}t_{2}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{9}{4}B_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\varPhi_{1},\varPhi_{2})+\displaystyle\frac{9}{2}\{Q_{1},P_{2}\}-\displaystyle\frac{9}{2}\{P_{1},Q_{2}\}+36r_{1}r_{2}+18t_{1}s_{2}+18s_{1}t_{2}.

Proof..

Since
(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
is simple, there exists
k\in C
such that

\displaystyle B_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(R_{1},R_{2})=k(R_{1},R_{2})_{8},\quad R_{i}\in(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}.

We will determine
k
. Let
R_{1}=R_{2}=(0,0,0,1,0,0)=:\tilde{1}
. Then we have

\displaystyle(\tilde{1},\tilde{1})_{8}=-8.

On the other hand, it follows from

\displaystyle(\mbox{\rm{ad}}\,\tilde{1})(\mbox{\rm{ad}}\,\tilde{1})R

\displaystyle=[\tilde{1},[\tilde{1},(\varPhi,P,Q,r,s,t)]\,],\,\,R\in(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}

\displaystyle=[\tilde{1},(0,P,-Q,0,2s,-2t)]

\displaystyle=(0,P,Q,0,4s,4t)

that

\displaystyle B_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(\tilde{1},\tilde{1})=\mbox{\rm{tr}}((\mbox{\rm{ad}}\tilde{1})(\mbox{\rm{ad}}\tilde{1}))=14\times 2+4\times 2=36.

Thus we have
k=-9/2
.

Therefore we have

\displaystyle B_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}(R_{1},R_{2})=-\displaystyle\frac{9}{2}(R_{1},R_{2})_{8}

and the remainders of formulas can be easily obtained.
∎

We move the determination of the root system and Dynkin diagram of the Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

We define a Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{8}
of
(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{8}=\left\{R_{8}=(\varPhi,0,0,r,0,0)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\begin{array}[]{l}\varPhi=\varPhi(\phi,0,0,\nu)\in\mathfrak{h}_{7},\\
\quad\phi=\tilde{T}_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}_{6},\\
\qquad T_{0}=\tau_{1}E_{1}+\tau_{2}E_{2}+\tau_{3}E_{3}\in(({\mathfrak{J}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}})^{C})_{0},\\
\qquad\qquad\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}+\tau_{3}=0,\tau_{i}\in C,\\
\quad\nu\in C,\\
r\in C\end{array}\right\}.

Then the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{8}
is a Cartan subalgebra of
(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
.

Theorem 6.2
.

The roots
\varDelta
of
(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
relative to
\mathfrak{h}_{8}
are given by

\displaystyle\varDelta=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\pm\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{3}),\pm\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{3}-\tau_{1}),\pm\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})\\[5.69054pt]
\pm(\tau_{1}+\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\pm(\tau_{2}+\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\pm(\tau_{3}+\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\\[5.69054pt]
\pm(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{1}-\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\pm(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{2}-\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\pm(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{3}-\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\nu),\\[5.69054pt]
\pm(\tau_{1}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\pm(\tau_{2}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\pm(\tau_{3}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\\[5.69054pt]
\pm(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{1}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\pm(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{2}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\pm(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{3}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\\[5.69054pt]
\pm(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{1}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\pm(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{2}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\pm(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau_{3}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\\[5.69054pt]
\pm(-\tau_{1}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\pm(-\tau_{2}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\pm(-\tau_{3}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\nu+r),\\[5.69054pt]
\pm(\nu+r),\pm(-\nu+r),\pm 2r\end{array}\right\}.

Proof..

The roots of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
are also the roots of
(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Indeed, let the root
\alpha
of
(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
and its associated root vector
\varPhi_{s}\in(\mathfrak{e}_{7,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}\subset(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Then we have

\displaystyle[R_{8},\varPhi_{s}]

\displaystyle=[(\varPhi,0,0,r,0,0)(\varPhi_{s},0,0,0,0,0)]

\displaystyle=([\varPhi,\varPhi_{s}],0,0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=(\alpha(\varPhi)\varPhi_{s},0,0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=\alpha(\varPhi)(\varPhi_{s},0,0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=\alpha(R)\varPhi_{s}.

We will determine the remainders of roots. We will show a few examples.
First, let
R_{8}=(\varPhi,0,0,r,0,0)\in\mathfrak{h}_{8}
and
{R^{-}}_{\dot{E}_{1}}:=(0,\dot{E}_{1},0,0,0,0)\in(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, where
\dot{E}_{1}:=(E_{1},0,0,0)\in(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Then it follows that

\displaystyle[R_{8},{R^{-}}_{\dot{E}_{1}}]

\displaystyle=[(\varPhi,0,0,r,0,0),(0,\dot{E}_{1},0,0,0,0)]

\displaystyle=(0,\varPhi\dot{E}_{1}+r\dot{E}_{1},0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=(0,\varPhi(\phi,0,0,\nu)(E_{1},0,0,0)+r(E_{1},0,0,0),0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=(0,(\phi-(1/3)\nu+r)E_{1},0,0,0),0,0,0,0),\,\,(\phi=\tilde{T}_{0})

\displaystyle=(0,((\tau_{1}-(1/3)\nu+r)E_{1},0,0,0),0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=(\tau_{1}-(1/3)\nu+r)(0,\dot{E}_{1},0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=(\tau_{1}-(1/3)\nu+r){R^{-}}_{\dot{E}_{1}},

that is,
[R_{8},{R^{-}}_{\dot{E}_{1}}]=(\tau_{1}-(1/3)\nu+r){R^{-}}_{\dot{E}_{1}}
. Hence we see that
\tau_{1}-(1/3)\nu+r
is a root and
(0,\dot{E}_{1},0,0,0,0)
is an associated root vector.
Next, let
{R^{-}}_{\dot{F}_{1}(1)}:=(0,\dot{F}_{1}(1),0,0,0,0)\in(\mathfrak{e}_{8,\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
, where
\dot{F}_{1}(1):=(F_{1}(1),0,0,0)\in(\mathfrak{P}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}})^{C}
. Then it follows that

\displaystyle[R_{8},{R^{-}}_{\dot{F}_{1}(1)}]

\displaystyle=[(\varPhi,0,0,r,0,0),(0,\dot{F}_{1}(1),0,0,0,0)]

\displaystyle=(0,\varPhi\dot{F}_{1}(1)+r\dot{F}_{1}(1),0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=(0,\varPhi(\phi,0,0,\nu)(F_{1}(1),0,0,0)+r(F_{1}(1),0,0,0),0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=(0,(\phi-(1/3)\nu+r)F_{1}(1),0,0,0),0,0,0,0),\,\,(\phi=\tilde{T}_{0})

\displaystyle=(0,(((1/2)(\tau_{2}+\tau_{3})-(1/3)\nu+r)F_{1}(1),0,0,0),0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=(0,(-(1/2)\tau_{1}-(1/3)\nu+r)\dot{F}_{1}(1),0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle=(-(1/2)\tau_{1}-(1/3)\nu+r){R^{-}}_{\dot{F}_{1}(1)},

that is,

is a root and
(0,\dot{F}_{1}(1),0,0,0,0)
